Re: 
Southern California Edison Company Docket No. ER27-___-000 –   
Dear Secretary Reese: 
On June 18, 2026, the Commission issued an Order Instituting Proceeding Under Section 
206 of the Federal Power Act (“FPA”) (“Order to Show Cause” or “Order”) regarding 
interconnections and transmission service for large loads.  The Order directed the California 
Independent System Operator (“CAISO”) and the Participating Transmission Owners (“PTOs”) 
to (1) show cause as to why their respective Tariffs remain just and reasonable and not unduly 
discriminatory or preferential; or (2) explain what changes to their respective Tariffs they believe 
would remedy the concerns if the Commission were to determine that the Tariff has in fact 
become unjust and unreasonable or unduly discriminatory or preferential and, therefore, 
proceeds to establish a replacement Tariff.   
Southern California Edison Company (“SCE”) supports the Commission’s efforts to 
ensure that large loads can be integrated into our transmission system in a manner that does not 
shift costs or cause harm to smaller customers.  In fact, if done properly, the integration of new 
load has the potential to both provide transmission cost relief and to enhance reliability to 
everyone on the grid.  SCE’s proposals herein set the groundwork to realize these benefits, while 
at the same time providing transparency and reasonable rules for integrating new large loads.
--- PAGE 2 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 2 
November 16, 2026  
 
2 
SCE, as a PTO, hereby submits this filing under FPA Section 205 with proposed changes 
to its Transmission Owner Tariff (“TO Tariff”) to address items identified in the Order.  This 
Transmittal Letter also addresses the Commission’s Briefing Questions at Section IV of the 
Order.  
I. 
BACKGROUND 
As SCE stated in its Request for Abeyance, many of the Commission’s identified 
concerns can only be resolved with complementary or, at a minimum, non-conflicting tariff 
provisions among the CAISO Tariff and the various TO Tariffs.  In order to respond to the 
deficiencies raised in the Order to Show Cause, SCE has had robust coordination with other 
California PTOs and the CAISO while developing the tariff changes described herein.    
Shortly after the Commission issued the Order to Show Cause, SCE began engaging with 
the CAISO and other stakeholders to coordinate its response.  SCE actively participated in the 
CAISO stakeholder process that produced the CAISO’s Large Loads Enhancements proposal.  
SCE attended the CAISO’s stakeholder meetings on its Straw Proposal and the CAISO Draft 
Final Proposal and submitted written comments on each, including comments on the definition 
of Large Load, the large load application process and interconnection study procedures, 
operational requirements, service agreements, and the CAISO’s draft tariff language.  SCE also 
continuously coordinated with the other PTOs, including Pacific Gas and Electric Company 
(“PG&E”) and San Diego Gas & Electric Company (“SDG&E”), and with the CAISO 
throughout the process to promote a fair and compatible application of requirements and 
procedures across the CAISO system, and to identify where the PTOs and the CAISO are aligned 
and where their positions differ.  
Ultimately, the CAISO has decided to defer to the various PTOs to adopt and implement 
TO Tariff changes to their load interconnection processes to respond to most of the deficiencies
--- PAGE 3 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 3 
November 16, 2026  
 
3 
preliminarily identified in the Order to Show Cause.1  This approach requires each PTO to 
develop their TO Tariff provisions on critical issues, including the application and study process; 
flexible transmission service; and cost-shifting mitigation.  SCE appreciates that the ISOs/RTOs 
have significant regional differences that must be recognized.  In light of the existing division of 
responsibilities between PTOs and the CAISO, SCE understands that the CAISO’s approach 
may be unique amongst ISOs/RTOs subject to the Commission’s Orders to Show Cause2 in 
having the PTOs address these issues in their own TO Tariffs.  In the absence of CAISO 
direction or tariff changes applicable to Eligible Customers taking transmission service on behalf 
of large load, SCE understands that there will likely be some diversity as to the details and 
substance of the various TO Tariff filings in response to the Order to Show Cause.  Each PTO 
starts with a unique tariff, and thus changes must necessarily conform to the unique features of 
each PTO tariff.  Despite this necessary diversity among TO Tariffs, the Commission’s goals in 
issuing the Order nevertheless can and should be reached.  As such, and particularly given the 
compressed timeline for responses to the Order to Show Cause, SCE believes uniformity in the 
PTOs’ tariffs should not be expected.  Nonetheless, SCE understands it is aligned with PG&E, 
SDG&E, and LS Power in requiring security or upfront payments for network upgrades triggered 
by large loads, as well as exit fees, to help protect against cost shifting due to speculative or 
departing loads.  SCE also understands that it and other PTOs are also implementing similar 
flexible interim interconnection proposals.  Moreover, SCE has reached agreement with the 
CAISO and other PTOs on the foundational issue of the definition of Large Load.

II. 
RESPONSE TO SHOW CAUSE ORDER 
In the Order to Show Cause, the Commission found that CAISO’s existing CAISO Tariff 
and/or the TO Tariffs preliminarily appear to be unjust, unreasonable, or unduly discriminatory 
or preferential.3  CAISO and the PTOs were directed to either file revised tariff language, or 
show cause as to why their tariffs remain just and reasonable and not unduly discriminatory or 
preferential without provisions addressing:  
(a) the application process, study procedures, and ongoing operational 
requirements that apply to Eligible Customers seeking transmission service on 
behalf of large loads; 
(b) additional transparency concerning the network upgrade costs to 
provide transmission service to Eligible Customers on behalf of large loads; and a 
pro forma cost recovery agreement between CAISO, the relevant transmission 
owner, and Eligible Customer taking transmission service on behalf of the large 
load to mitigate the risk of cost shifting among transmission customers, and a 
mechanism to ensure such payments are appropriately credited toward 
transmission owners’ transmission revenue requirements consistent with the 
Commission’s cost-of-service regulations;  
(c) the rates, terms, and conditions of service that apply to co-location 
arrangements;  
(d) transmission services that reflect Eligible Customers taking 
transmission service on behalf of co-located loads, load with behind the meter 
generation, and flexible large loads that are willing and able to limit their use of 
the transmission system under certain conditions; and 
 
3  Order at P 3.
--- PAGE 5 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 5 
November 16, 2026  
 
5 
(e) the rates, terms, and conditions of service applicable to interconnection 
customers serving electrically proximate large load or co-located load[.] 
In this filing, SCE proposes revisions to its TO Tariff in response to the concerns 
identified in the Order to Show Cause that are not being fully addressed by the CAISO in its own 
CAISO Tariff revisions.  In coordination with the CAISO’s expected revisions to its own CAISO 
Tariff, SCE’s tariff revisions will connect large loads to the grid more efficiently, while also 
helping to shield existing ratepayers throughout the CAISO region from transmission costs 
linked to those large loads.  
Currently, SCE’s TO Tariff governs the interconnection of certain loads to SCE’s 
transmission facilities that are under the operational control of the CAISO.  Specifically, SCE’s 
TO Tariff was drafted in order to facilitate the interconnection of other transmission owners’ 
facilities to SCE’s transmission system as well as the interconnection of Load-Serving Entities’ 
(“LSEs”) transmission facilities to SCE’s facilities.  In this filing, the body of SCE’s existing TO 
Tariff regarding Eligible Customer interconnections remains largely unchanged.  Instead, SCE 
proposes to create a new Appendix titled the Large Load Interconnection Procedures (“LLIP”) to 
augment existing TO Tariff interconnection and cost responsibility sections with new provisions 
specifically tailored to the transmission interconnection of both Eligible Customers serving 
Large Loads and Large Loads that obtain their retail transmission service from SCE.  The new 
LLIP will be used when such entities seek to interconnect to SCE’s transmission system; for all 
other interconnections under the TO Tariff, SCE’s existing TO Tariff provisions will apply.   In 
developing the LLIP, SCE used Commission-approved Wholesale Distribution Access Tariff 
(“WDAT”) generator interconnection procedures as a starting point and revised the procedures 
where necessary to accommodate load requests.

Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission

November 16, 2026

A.  APPLICATION PROCESS, STUDY PROCEDURES, AND ONGOING 
 OPERATIONAL REQUIREMENTS 
Order to Show Cause Section A (“Transmission Service to Eligible Customers on Behalf 
of Large Loads”) preliminarily found that CAISO’s Tariff and/or the TO Tariffs appear to be 
unjust and unreasonable because they lack provisions that address, with sufficient clarity and 
consistency, how CAISO and/or the Participating Transmission Owners will timely study (i.e., 
within 60-90 days of receiving the request) the provision of transmission service to Eligible 
Customers on behalf of large loads.4  Regarding the definition of large load, the Order 
preliminarily found that the CAISO’s Tariff and/or the TO Tariffs appear to be unjust and 
unreasonable because they lack a definition of large load, as a new category of load, including a 
load size and interconnection voltage threshold, which should be based on the characteristics of 
CAISO’s transmission system.  The Commission ordered CAISO and/or the PTOs to explain 
whether their tariffs remain just and reasonable without such a definition or propose tariff 
revisions establishing their own definition of large load.5  The Commission was also concerned 
that CAISO’s existing Tariff and/or the TO Tariffs fail to deter speculative or duplicative 
requests for transmission service.6  Finally, the Commission suggested that a pro forma 
agreement to memorialize certain operational terms to support transmission system reliability, 
including the terms of any flexible transmission service arrangement, would be appropriate for 
inclusion in the CAISO Tariff and/or PTO Tariffs.7 
1. 
Tariff Changes to Incorporate a Definition of Large Load 
In Paragraph 57 of the Order, the Commission found that the CAISO and TO Tariffs lack 
a definition of large load and directed that an appropriate definition, including a load size and 
 
4  Id. at P 38. 
5  Id. at P 57. 
6 
Id. at P 55. 
7 
Id. at P 64.
--- PAGE 7 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 7 
November 16, 2026  
 
7 
interconnection voltage threshold, be based on the characteristics of CAISO’s transmission 
system.  The Commission suggested that it would be reasonable to define a large load as a new 
commercial or industrial customer, located at a single site behind one or more points of 
interconnection, that has a peak load of 50 MW or greater, interconnects to the transmission 
system at a voltage level greater than 69 kV, and is not part of a co-location arrangement. 
To satisfy this directive, SCE proposes to incorporate the CAISO’s definition of “Large 
Load” into its TO Tariff.  (See TO Tariff Section 3, stating that capitalized terms not defined in 
the TO Tariff shall be defined in the CAISO Tariff).  Under the CAISO’s proposed tariff 
language, a “Large Load” is “[a]n End User located at a single site behind one or more points of 
interconnection to the CAISO Controlled Grid, and that has a peak load of 50 MW or greater.”  
SCE participated in the CAISO’s stakeholder process and supports this definition. 
The CAISO’s proposed definition as incorporated into SCE’S TO Tariff is just and 
reasonable and responsive to the Order for three reasons.  First, it adopts the 50 MW peak load 
threshold suggested by the Commission, which the CAISO adopted and which the other PTOs 
now support.  Second, because the definition applies to an End User located at a single site 
behind one or more points of interconnection to the CAISO Controlled Grid, it is based on the 
characteristics of CAISO’s transmission system, and it applies only to loads that interconnect to 
the transmission facilities placed under the CAISO’s Operational Control and governed by the 
TO Tariff.  Loads interconnected to SCE’s distribution and sub-transmission systems are subject 
to the jurisdiction of the California Public Utilities Commission and are therefore outside the 
scope of the definition.  Third, a uniform definition across the CAISO Tariff and the TO Tariffs 
will provide clarity and consistency and avoid conflicting definitions of the same term.  The 
Large Load Interconnection Procedures, the Large Load Study Process Agreement, and the 
Transmission Security and Reliability Agreement described below apply to Large Loads as so 
defined.
--- PAGE 8 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 8 
November 16, 2026  
 
8 
SCE notes that the CAISO has stated that it may refine the definition as NERC develops 
similar terms for computational loads.  In the event the CAISO changes its definition in the 
future, SCE expects to conform its definition to maintain consistency across the tariffs. 
2. 
Tariff Changes to Update Application Process 
Consistent with the CAISO’s position, SCE proposes to continue to utilize the current 
framework of shared responsibilities among the CAISO and the PTOs for the interconnection of 
large loads to SCE’s transmission system.  Under that framework, SCE is largely responsible for 
processing applications and studying new large loads, while the CAISO remains responsible for 
reviewing those studies and ensuring alignment with transmission planning activities. 
The Order preliminarily found that the TO Tariffs lack provisions that address, with 
sufficient clarity and consistency, how the PTOs will timely study (i.e., within 60-90 days of 
receiving the request) the provision of transmission service to Eligible Customers on behalf of 
large loads.  SCE’s proposed Large Load Interconnection Procedures (“LLIP”), a new Appendix 
XV to the TO Tariff, address this concern by establishing a defined, time-bound application and 
study process for Large Loads.  Although technically an “interconnection process,” the study 
process adopted ensures that power can be delivered to the Large Load under the CAISO’s 
unique transmission regime. 
Critical to the LLIP are its eligibility criteria.  The CAISO has expressed concern that 
existing provisions in the TO Tariffs apply only to a subset of Eligible Customers.8  SCE 
understands this language to acknowledge that not all transmission interconnections proceed 
through an Eligible Customer.  Specifically, when SCE interconnects large loads that SCE will 
serve at retail, the TO Tariff does not require SCE to apply for an interconnection to itself as an 
Eligible Customer under the TO Tariff.  A plain reading of the Order could suggest that SCE’s 
 
8 
Id. at P 83.
--- PAGE 9 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 9 
November 16, 2026  
 
9 
own retail transmission customers should be excluded from obligations under the Order since 
SCE is not its own Eligible Customer.  However, SCE does not believe that the Commission 
meant to exclude SCE’s own transmission-connected retail transmission customers from 
requirements that are within FERC’s jurisdiction and that concern the FERC-jurisdictional 
transmission system.  For instance, SCE believes that it should be obligated to engage in the 
same study requirements and timelines regardless of whether the Large Load is SCE’s own retail 
transmission customer or whether the Large Load is provided retail transmission service 
(including bundled service) by another Eligible Customer, such as a Utility Distribution 
Company or MSS Operator.  SCE’s LLIP thus applies the same transmission-related 
requirements to SCE’s own Large Load retail transmission customers as it does to Eligible 
Customers seeking to interconnect on behalf of Large Loads, subject to exceptions based on 
areas such as the evaluation of distribution upgrades for SCE’s own retail transmission 
customers that are exclusively within state law jurisdiction.   
Accordingly, SCE has defined the Large Load Interconnection Customer to whom the 
LLIP applies as “an Eligible Customer that proposes to interconnect and sell retail transmission 
service to a Large Load except for when the Large Load Owner seeking to interconnect the 
Large Load with the Participating TO’s Transmission System will be a retail transmission 
customer of the Participating TO, in which case the Large Load Owner shall be the Large Load 
Interconnection Customer.”  (LLIP Section 2.)  To preserve jurisdictional boundaries, Large 
Load Owners who are retail transmission customers of SCE will still seek interconnection under 
the Local Regulatory Authority.  However, the FERC-jurisdictional transmission-related 
procedures and obligations in the LLIP will also apply to those Large Loads that are SCE’s retail 
transmission customers.  In response to the Commission’s Briefing Question at P 123, SCE notes 
that the LLIP also preserves existing commercial arrangements because it is only applicable to 
new Large Load requests for interconnection submitted after the Commission approves the TO
--- PAGE 10 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 10 
November 16, 2026  
 
10 
Tariff changes.  (LLIP Section 1.2, “This LLIP applies to all Large Loads seeking 
interconnection to Participating TO’s transmission system after the effective date of this 
Appendix XV to the TO Tariff.”)      
Under the LLIP, a Large Load Interconnection Customer begins the process by 
submitting an Interconnection Request using the form included as Attachment 1 to the LLIP, 
together with an Interconnection Study Deposit of $250,000, a Commercial Readiness Deposit 
equal to two times the study deposit, the technical data required by Appendix A to Attachment 1, 
and a demonstration of Site Control (LLIP Section 4.2.1).  Within 10 Business Days of receipt, 
SCE, as the Participating TO, notifies the customer whether the request is valid, and a customer 
with a deficient request must cure it within the time provided in LLIP Section 4.3.2 (LLIP 
Sections 4.3.1 and 4.3.2).  The Participating TO may defer the start of the study where higher-
queued projects must be studied first, in which case it must notify the customer and provide 
monthly status updates (LLIP Section 4.4).  Within 15 Business Days after the request is deemed 
valid, the Participating TO schedules a Scoping Meeting with the customer and, if applicable, the 
CAISO, to discuss Service Commencement Dates and interconnection options and to determine 
the Point of Interconnection (LLIP Section 4.5).  Within 5 Business Days after the Scoping 
Meeting, the Participating TO provides the Large Load Study Process Agreement, which is 
included as Attachment 2 to the LLIP, and the customer must execute it within 30 Calendar Days 
after the Scoping Meeting or its Interconnection Request will be deemed withdrawn (LLIP 
Section 4.5).  The agreement outlines the scope of the study (through the assumptions in its 
Appendix A), provides a non-binding good faith estimate of the study cost, and memorializes the 
customer’s obligation to pay the actual costs of the Interconnection Studies and to increase its 
Commercial Readiness Deposit after the final study report.  It also permits the Participating TO 
to share study results with the CAISO, any Affected System Operator, and the Western 
Electricity Coordinating Council.
--- PAGE 11 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 11 
November 16, 2026  
 
11 
The Participating TO then performs a serial Large Load Interconnection Study, which 
consists of a short circuit analysis, a power flow analysis, an electromagnetic transient (“EMT”) 
screening. The study identifies a non-binding good faith estimate of the scope and cost of the 
Participating TO’s Interconnection Facilities, Distribution Upgrades, and Large Load Network 
Upgrades, as applicable, that are required as a result of the Interconnection Request, and will also 
identify the need for any additional studies (LLIP Section 4.6.1.1).  Where the Large Load will 
be a retail transmission customer of the Participating TO, the study evaluates the impact of the 
proposed interconnection on the safety and reliability of the Participating TO’s Transmission 
System and, if applicable, any Affected System, and does not evaluate Distribution Upgrades, 
which are governed by the Local Regulatory Authority.  The study will also consider the use of 
alternative transmission technologies, including dynamic line ratings, advanced power flow 
control devices, advanced conductors, and transmission switching when evaluating transmission 
solutions (LLIP Section 4.6.1.1).  The Participating TO shares the study results with the CAISO 
for review and concurrence under Section 25.7 of the CAISO Tariff, consistent with the 
CAISO’s role in reviewing those studies and ensuring alignment with transmission planning, as 
described above (LLIP Sections 4.6 and 4.6.1.2).  If the Participating TO identifies a need for 
additional studies to comply with Applicable Reliability Standards, such as stability, variable 
load impact, or EMT analysis, they are performed as part of the scope of work under a new pro 
forma Transmission Security and Reliability Agreement (“TSRA”) at the customer’s cost (LLIP 
Section 4.6.3). 
The LLIP provides firm timelines that are responsive to the Commission’s 60–90-day 
expectation.  The Participating TO will use Reasonable Efforts to complete the Large Load 
Interconnection Study within 90 Calendar Days after execution of the Large Load Study Process 
Agreement (LLIP Section 4.6.1.2).  A Report Meeting among the Participating TO, the CAISO 
(if applicable), and the Large Load Interconnection Customer must be held within 10 Business
--- PAGE 12 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 12 
November 16, 2026  
 
12 
Days after the study report is provided to the customer to discuss the results, including assigned 
cost responsibility (LLIP Section 4.6.1.3).  If the Participating TO will not meet the required 
timeframe, it must notify the customer of the schedule status and provide an estimated 
completion date with an explanation of why additional time is required (LLIP Section 4.6.1.2).  
After the Report Meeting, the customer may submit written comments, and the Participating TO 
will determine whether a revised study report is necessary and issue any revised report within 15 
Business Days after the Report Meeting (LLIP Section 4.6.1.2). 
The LLIP also includes provisions to ensure that Participating TO study resources are 
directed to commercially viable projects, which is discussed in Section II.A.3 below.  Within 30 
Calendar Days after receiving the final study report, the customer must increase its Commercial 
Readiness Deposit to 20% of its Large Load Network Upgrade cost assignment identified in the 
study, or its Interconnection Request will be deemed withdrawn (LLIP Section 4.6.1.4). 
Where the study shows that the full amount of a Large Load’s request cannot be 
accommodated until network upgrades are completed, the LLIP allows the customer to request 
an optional Flexible Interim Large Load Interconnection (“FILI”) Study after the Large Load 
Interconnection Study, to determine whether the Large Load may interconnect on a limited basis 
in the interim (LLIP Section 4.6.2).  If the customer elects a FILI Study, the Participating TO 
will complete within 60 Calendar Days.  This option is discussed in Section II.C below. 
Finally, NERC is still developing operational standards and study requirements that may 
affect the studies SCE needs to conduct. Further refinement of the LLIP may be required to 
conform with NERC standards. 
3. 
Tariff Changes to Deter Speculative Large Load 
The Order directed the CAISO and PTOs to address how their tariffs deter speculative or 
duplicative large load interconnection requests, which can consume study resources and create
--- PAGE 13 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 13 
November 16, 2026  
 
13 
uncertainty in transmission planning.  The LLIP addresses this concern by requiring each Large 
Load Interconnection Customer to demonstrate commercial readiness and financial commitment 
at each stage of the process, and by imposing financial consequences on customers that withdraw 
after study resources have been committed. 
First, to be deemed valid, an Interconnection Request must be accompanied by an 
Interconnection Study Deposit of $250,000 and a Commercial Readiness Deposit equal to two 
times the study deposit, in the form of an irrevocable letter of credit, cash, a surety bond, or other 
security reasonably acceptable to the Participating TO (LLIP Sections 4.2.1 and 4.7.1).  The 
Interconnection Study Deposit is applied to the prudent costs incurred by the Participating TO, 
the CAISO, or third parties to perform and administer the study (LLIP Section 4.2.1.1).  The 
Commercial Readiness Deposit is refundable in accordance with LLIP Section 3.10.1, although 
the Participating TO may hold it until the Service Commencement Date, and it is retained as 
financial security for any Withdrawal Penalty.  Within 30 Calendar Days after receiving the final 
study report, the customer must increase its Commercial Readiness Deposit to 20% of its Large 
Load Network Upgrade cost assignment identified in the study, or its Interconnection Request is 
deemed withdrawn (LLIP Section 4.6.1.4).  As a result, the financial commitment required of a 
customer increases as the study identifies the upgrades the customer’s load would require. 
Second, the LLIP requires the customer to demonstrate Site Control at the time of its 
Interconnection Request (LLIP Section 4.2.1).  Site Control means the exclusive land right to 
develop, construct, operate, and maintain the Large Load over the term of its expected operation, 
which may be shown by ownership of, a leasehold interest in, or a right to develop a site of 
sufficient size; an option to purchase or acquire a leasehold on such a site; or other 
documentation that clearly demonstrates the customer’s right to exclusively occupy such a site 
(LLIP Section 2).  Requiring evidence of control over the site where the load will be located 
screens out requests from customers that have no realistic ability to build the project, and helps
--- PAGE 14 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 14 
November 16, 2026  
 
14 
prevent multiple, duplicative requests for the same site.  In addition, a customer that proposes a 
Service Commencement Date more than seven years after its request must demonstrate that the 
required engineering, permitting, and construction will take longer than seven years (LLIP 
Section 3.7.1). 
Third, the LLIP imposes a Withdrawal Penalty that increases as the process advances 
(LLIP Section 3.10).  A customer that withdraws its Interconnection Request, or is deemed 
withdrawn, before the Large Load Interconnection Study begins is not subject to a Withdrawal 
Penalty.  If the customer withdraws, is deemed withdrawn, or otherwise does not achieve its 
Service Commencement Date at any point after the study begins, its Withdrawal Penalty will be 
the greater of (1) its $250,000 study deposit or (2) an amount determined by the stage at which 
the withdrawal occurs: (a) two times the actual cost of the study, if the customer withdraws or is 
deemed withdrawn during the study or after receipt of the study report; (b) 10% of its estimated 
Large Load Network Upgrade costs, if it withdraws or is deemed withdrawn after receipt of the 
draft TSRA but before it executes the TSRA or requests that the TSRA be filed unexecuted; or 
(c) once it has executed the TSRA or requested that the TSRA be filed unexecuted, the amount 
calculated under the TSRA, which, before the Service Commencement Date, is the greater of 
20% of its estimated Large Load Network Upgrade costs or the actual cost of any abandoned 
Large Load Network Upgrade facilities (LLIP Section 3.10.1(c); TSRA Appendix B).  The 
penalty does not apply if the withdrawal has no material impact on the cost or timing of any 
equal or later queued request, or if the customer withdraws after receiving a study report in 
which its assigned Large Load Network Upgrade costs have increased by more than 25% over its 
preceding study report or by more than 100% over its initial Large Load Interconnection Study 
report (LLIP Section 3.10).  A customer also is not subject to the penalty if it withdraws within 
30 Calendar Days after an additional study increases those costs by more than 25% (LLIP 
Section 4.6.3).  Once service begins, the customer is instead subject to the Exit Fee and
--- PAGE 15 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 15 
November 16, 2026  
 
15 
Minimum Annual Revenue Obligation provisions of the TSRA, as described in Section II.B 
below. 
Together, these provisions ensure that customers requesting study resources commit 
capital, provide evidence that the Large Load Owner controls the proposed site where the Large 
Load will be located, and bear the cost of withdrawing, which discourages speculative or 
duplicative requests. 
4. 
Tariff Changes to Create a New Pro Forma Transmission Security and 
Reliability Agreement 
The Order directed the CAISO and PTOs to address the ongoing operational 
requirements that apply to Eligible Customers taking transmission service on behalf of large 
loads, and to consider an agreement that memorializes the terms on which such service is 
provided.  SCE proposes to address these requirements through the new pro forma TSRA, 
included as Attachment 3 to the LLIP.  The TSRA is a standard-form agreement between the 
Large Load Interconnection Customer and SCE, as the Participating TO, that SCE tenders, 
together with draft appendices, after the Large Load Interconnection Study is complete (LLIP 
Section 5.1).  It consolidates in one agreement the facilities, operational, reliability, and financial 
security terms applicable to the Large Load.  The TSRA is executed only by the customer and 
SCE; the CAISO’s role in SCE’s new flexible interim interconnection service, FILI is reflected 
through a new Operating Conditions Agreement for Flexible Load Interconnection (“FILI 
Agreement”) among the customer, SCE, and the CAISO (LLIP Attachment 4), discussed below 
and in Section II.C.  The TSRA has a term of 15 years from its effective date (TSRA Article 
2.2).  It consists of twenty-nine Articles, covering, among other things, term and termination 
(Article 2), regulatory filings (Article 3), scope of service (Article 4), engineering, procurement, 
and construction (Article 5), testing and inspection (Article 6), metering (Article 7),
--- PAGE 16 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 16 
November 16, 2026  
 
16 
communications (Article 8), operations (Article 9), maintenance (Article 10), performance 
obligations and financial security (Article 11), invoices (Article 12), emergencies (Article 13), 
default (Article 17), indemnity and insurance (Article 18), confidentiality (Article 22), 
information requirements and audit rights (Articles 24 and 25), and disputes (Article 27), and of 
seven appendices (Appendices A through G). 
The TSRA addresses ongoing operational and reliability requirements in several respects.  
It requires each party to comply with Applicable Reliability Standards and Electric Reliability 
Organization requirements and to share the information reasonably required for the other party to 
do so (TSRA Article 9.1).  It requires the Participating TO and the CAISO to operate and control 
the Participating TO’s Transmission System in a safe and reliable manner, and it allows the 
Participating TO and the CAISO to provide operating instructions to the Large Load 
Interconnection Customer consistent with the TSRA and applicable operating protocols (TSRA 
Article 9.3).  It requires the customer to operate the Large Load in a safe and reliable manner and 
in accordance with the requirements of its Balancing Authority Area, as set forth in Appendix D, 
and prohibits the customer from operating the Large Load in parallel with the Participating TO’s 
Transmission System without the Participating TO’s prior written approval (TSRA Article 9.3).  
The customer must also comply with the Participating TO’s Interconnection Handbook (TSRA 
Article 5.6.4); install a Remote Terminal Unit and provide telemetry before the Service 
Commencement Date (TSRA Article 8); design the Large Load to maintain a power factor 
between 0.99 leading and 0.97 lagging at the Point of Interconnection (TSRA Article 9.5.1); 
provide frequency and voltage ride-through capability (TSRA Article 9.6.1); and install system 
protection facilities and other protective devices (TSRA Article 9.6.3).  The TSRA also provides 
for the exchange of switching and tagging rules and of disturbance analysis data, including 
oscillography, protective relay targets, breaker operations, and sequence of events records 
(TSRA Article 9); sets maintenance obligations for both parties (TSRA Article 10); and
--- PAGE 17 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 17 
November 16, 2026  
 
17 
addresses Emergency Conditions, including the Participating TO’s authority to reduce service to, 
or disconnect, the Large Load (TSRA Article 13).   
The TSRA also provides for the design, procurement, and construction of facilities 
needed to serve the Large Load (TSRA Article 5).  The Participating TO uses Reasonable Efforts 
to complete its Interconnection Facilities, Distribution Upgrades, and Large Load Network 
Upgrades by the dates in Appendix C (Article 5.1).  Design and procurement begin once the 
Interconnection Studies are complete, the Participating TO has received the customer’s written 
authorization to proceed by the date specified in Appendix C, and the customer has provided the 
required security (Article 5.2).  Construction begins once any required regulatory approvals and 
real property rights have been obtained and the customer has given its written authorization to 
proceed with construction (Article 5.3).  That said, the (generally applicable) seven-year limit 
from service request to Service Commencement Date means that the customer must provide such 
written authorization at such a time that leaves sufficient time to complete facilities before this 
seven-year period runs out.  The TSRA also requires the customer to provide specifications for 
its own interconnection facilities, initially at least 180 Calendar Days, and in final form at least 
90 Calendar Days, before the Service Commencement Date, so that the Participating TO can 
confirm they are compatible with the Transmission System (Article 5.6.1). 
For customers that elect FILI, TSRA Article 9.7 provides that the conditions and 
limitations applicable to FILI service, including curtailments, are specified in Appendix E to the 
TSRA.  In addition, such customers must execute the FILI Agreement included as Attachment 4 
to the LLIP with SCE and the CAISO before the Service Commencement Date (LLIP Section 
4.6.2.1).  That agreement describes the circumstances in which the CAISO or SCE may 
temporarily curtail the Large Load, and the mechanism that will be used to do so, consistent with 
Section 25.7.2 of the CAISO Tariff.  FILI is discussed further in Section II.C below.

The TSRA includes seven appendices (Appendices A through G) that are completed for 
each Large Load Interconnection Customer.  Under LLIP Section 5.1, within 30 Calendar Days 
after the Report Meeting for the final Large Load Interconnection Study report (or the final FILI 
Study report, if applicable), the Participating TO tenders a draft TSRA together with draft 
appendices, and the customer has 30 Calendar Days to provide written comments or notify the 
Participating TO that it has none.  The parties may negotiate the appendices for up to 60 
Calendar Days after the final study report; the customer must then execute the TSRA, request 
that it be filed unexecuted, or initiate dispute resolution, or its Interconnection Request is deemed 
withdrawn, and the Participating TO may declare an impasse and file the TSRA unexecuted with 
the Commission within 21 Calendar Days (LLIP Sections 5.2 and 5.3).  The appendices are 
described below. 
Appendix A (Description of Participating TO’s Interconnection Facilities, Large Load 
Network Upgrades, Distribution Upgrades, Costs, Payments and Interconnection Financial 
Security) describes the scope of work for the Large Load Interconnection Customer’s 
Interconnection Facilities and for the Participating TO’s Interconnection Facilities, Distribution 
Upgrades, and Large Load Network Upgrades identified in the Large Load Interconnection 
Study, together with the associated cost estimates, payments, and security.  The LLIP also 
defines the Point of Interconnection and the Point of Change of Ownership by reference to 
Appendix A.   
Appendix B (Requirements for Mitigation of Potential Cost Shifting Related to New 
Transmission Facilities) is the central cost-shifting protection in the TSRA, as described in 
Section II.B below, and it is the appendix to which TSRA Articles 11.3, 11.4.2.1, and 11.4.2.3 
refer.   
Appendix C (Milestones) sets the dates for the design, procurement, and construction of 
the Participating TO’s Interconnection Facilities, Distribution Upgrades, and Large Load

Federal Energy Regulatory Commission

Network Upgrades, against which the Participating TO uses Reasonable Efforts to complete its 
work (TSRA Article 5.1).  It also sets the dates by which the customer must provide written 
authorization to proceed with design, procurement, and construction and post the required 
Interconnection Financial Security (TSRA Articles 5.2, 5.3, and 11.4).  If the Participating TO 
reasonably expects that it will not meet a milestone date, it must promptly notify the customer in 
writing. 
Appendix D (Interconnection Details) sets out the interconnection details and operating 
requirements with which the customer must operate the Large Load, which are updated as those 
requirements change (TSRA Article 9.3).   
Appendix E (Flexible Interim Load Interconnection Conditions) applies only to 
customers that elect FILI.  It is where the conditions and limitations of the interim service, 
including curtailments, are specified under TSRA Section 9.8, and it is used together with the 
FILI Agreement in Attachment 4 to the LLIP.   
Appendix F (Security Arrangements Details) addresses the infrastructure security of the 
Participating TO’s Transmission System and Distribution System equipment and its operations 
and control hardware and software.  It states the expectation that the CAISO, transmission 
providers, market participants, and Large Load Interconnection Customers interconnected to the 
Participating TO’s Transmission System will comply with infrastructure-protection 
recommendations and best practices, including physical, operational, and cyber-security 
practices. 
B.  COST SHIFTING MITIGATION 
Order Section B (“Cost Shifting Risk Among Transmission Customers”) preliminarily 
found that CAISO’s Tariff and/or the TO Tariffs appear to be unjust and unreasonable because 
they lack adequate mechanisms to mitigate the risk of cost shifting among transmission
--- PAGE 20 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 20 
November 16, 2026  
 
20 
customers, which may result in unjust and unreasonable rates for transmission service.9  In 
particular, the Commission was concerned about the apparent lack of (1) transparency regarding 
the assignment of network upgrades, and their associated costs, that are needed to provide 
transmission service to Eligible Customers on behalf of large loads; and (2) a pro forma cost 
recovery agreement to help ensure that Eligible Customers bear the risk and are ultimately 
responsible for costs incurred to provide transmission service, including the cost of network 
upgrades.10 
The Order directed the CAISO and the PTOs to explain whether the tariffs remain just 
and reasonable without (1) robust, accurate, and systematic provision of data on CAISO’s 
website in a single location, and in an easily accessible format that is searchable and allows users 
to filter the data, regarding the cost for network upgrades needed to provide transmission service 
to Eligible Customers on behalf of large loads; and (2) a pro forma cost recovery agreement 
between CAISO, the relevant transmission owner, and the Eligible Customer taking transmission 
service on behalf of a large load, or to propose tariff revisions to provide additional transparency 
and establish such a pro forma cost recovery agreement.11 
In response to the Order, the CAISO is not proposing changes to its Tariff to address cost 
shifting risk among transmission customers.  Instead, as stated in the CAISO Draft Final 
Proposal, it believes it is prudent for cost allocation rules to be set forth in the PTO tariffs rather 
than the CAISO tariff.12   In the absence of CAISO tariff changes, changes to each PTO’s tariff 
will be required in order to mitigate cost shifting.  Accordingly, SCE is proposing revisions to its 
TO Tariff to mitigate cost shifting risks associated with the interconnection of large loads to 
SCE’s transmission system. SCE understand the general framework and Large Load obligations 
 
9  Id. at P 65. 
10  Id. 
11  Id. at P 66. 
12  CAISO Draft Final Proposal, p. 44.
--- PAGE 21 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary

November 16, 2026  
 
21 
of SCE’s proposal are aligned with PG&E’s proposal.  Each Large Load Interconnection 
Customer will be subject to these cost obligations. 
1. 
Tariff Changes to Mitigate the Risk of Cost Shifting for Transmission 
Facilities to Serve Large Load 
The cost obligation provisions of SCE’s proposal are found in the LLIP and in the pro 
forma Transmission Security and Reliability Agreement (“TSRA”) (Attachment 3 to the LLIP).  
As discussed above in Section II.A.2, SCE understands that the Commission’s intent was not to 
exclude SCE’s own retail transmission customers from new FERC-jurisdictional, transmission-
related obligations associated with transmission service to large loads (including obligations 
related to the avoidance of cost shifting).  SCE’s LLIP transmission security requirements thus 
apply to either an Eligible Customer seeking to interconnect a Large Load, or where the Large 
Load Owner is SCE’s retail transmission customer, the Large Load Owner.   
Obligating Large Load Owners who are SCE’s retail transmission customers to comply 
with cost shifting mitigation requirements is necessary in order to achieve the Commission’s 
goals of reducing the risk of cost shifting among transmission customers.  As noted above in 
Section II.A.2, SCE does not interconnect to its own TO Tariff as an Eligible Customer of itself 
when interconnecting its own transmission retail customers to SCE’s transmission system.  Even 
if SCE were, hypothetically, determined to be an Eligible Customer of itself, any cost obligations 
resulting from the construction of new SCE transmission facilities that are assigned to SCE 
would flow into SCE’s Formula Rate and thus, into the Transmission Access Charge (“TAC”) 
paid by all CAISO participants.  Thus, obligating only SCE (as a fictional Eligible Customer) to 
pay transmission network upgrade costs would not satisfy the requirements of the Order to Show 
Cause to help prevent cost shifts among transmission customers since all of those costs would 
end up in the TAC regardless.  In the absence of any state law obligation or voluntary
--- PAGE 22 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 22 
November 16, 2026  
 
22 
agreement,13 SCE believes that obligating the Large Load Owner who is SCE’s retail 
transmission customer to provide cost shift mitigation security is thus required to comply with 
the Commission’s Order. 
SCE’s proposal does not impermissibly invade the Local Regulatory Authority’s 
jurisdiction over retail customers, as it is clear that the Commission retains jurisdiction over the 
allocation of certain transmission costs.  For instance, the Commission determines transmission 
rates for SCE’s retail rate classes in ruling on SCE’s Formula Rate, which is part of SCE’s TO 
Tariff.  As discussed in Section II.A.2 above, SCE’s proposed tariff revisions align with 
jurisdictional boundaries, given that the Large Load Owners who are SCE’s retail transmission 
customers still seek interconnection through state law tariffs.  The TSRA does not supplant those 
state law tariffs, but layers in additional cost provisions that are solely directed at FERC-
jurisdictional costs for transmission system upgrades.  Allocation for distribution system costs 
and distribution retail rates remains exclusively with the Local Regulatory Authority.       
The TSRA mitigates cost shifting for network upgrades through provisions located in 
TSRA Article 11 and Appendix B in three ways: (1) security for Large Load Network Upgrades 
triggered by the Large Load Interconnection Customer, and as applicable, any additional 
upgrades identified by the CAISO; (2) a 15-year minimum transmission revenue obligation 
backed by 5 years of security, which is meant to protect against stranded asset costs of broader 
network upgrades; and (3) exit fees for early termination.  Collectively, these provisions help 
mitigate the potential for cost shifts to other transmission customers arising out of the 
interconnection of large loads where the large load does not materialize as planned or is not 
developed at all.14  
 
13 Currently no state law tariff requires SCE’s retail transmission customers to provide security or 
payments for transmission network upgrades that are necessary to serve their load.  
14 Order at P 82.
--- PAGE 23 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 23 
November 16, 2026  
 
23 
First, the Large Load Interconnection Customer must provide Interconnection Financial 
Security at least 30 Calendar Days before work begins on the Large Load Network Upgrades 
attributable to it (TSRA Section 11.4.2.1).  Large Load Network Upgrades are defined as “the 
additions, modifications, and upgrades to the CAISO Controlled Grid at or beyond the Point of 
Interconnection necessary to serve one or more Large Loads safely and reliably that are 
identified through the Large Load Interconnection Study and sent to the CAISO for concurrence 
pursuant to ISO Tariff Section 25.7.”  (LLIP Section 2).  That security equals the estimated cost 
of the upgrades, including engineering, procurement, construction, and overhead, and increases if 
the scope changes.  In recognition of the reduced need for security to protect against cost shifting 
once the large load customer begins paying transmission rates, once retail service begins, the 
Large Load Interconnection Customer may reduce its associated security annually by 75% of the 
amount of MWh counted as Gross Load of the Large Load Interconnection Customer associated 
with withdrawals by the Large Load multiplied by the Regional Access Charge.  Any balance 
remaining after fifteen years is drawn down and credited under SCE’s Formula Rate via the 
Transmission Revenue Balancing Account Adjustment (TO Tariff Article 3.106).  Likewise, 
where the CAISO determines that additional cost-shifting protection is needed concerning shared 
network upgrade costs, the costs for such upgrades will be included in the security requirement.  
(TSRA Section 2, defining “ISO-Identified Network Upgrades.”)  In the case where the Large 
Load Interconnection Customer is expected to drive significant additional transmission costs, the 
identification of ISO-Identified Network Upgrades will allow for additional cost-shifting 
protection.   
Second, Appendix B imposes a Minimum Annual Revenue Obligation (“MARO”) so that 
the Large Load contributes to the transmission revenue requirement even if it does not ramp as 
planned.  Each year’s MARO equals the Minimum Obligation Capacity (in MW) for that year,
--- PAGE 24 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 24 
November 16, 2026  
 
24 
times the hours in the year, times 80% and 75%,15 times the Regional Access Charge.  The rates 
are fixed for fifteen years, and a fifteen-year Minimum Capacity Schedule, reaching the 
customer’s Requested Maximum Load by Year 5, sets the capacity.  A delay in the load’s ramp 
does not postpone scheduled increases.  The MARO is intended to cover at least some of the 
transmission network costs associated with transmission service to the Large Load 
Interconnection Customer that are not identified as Large Load Network Upgrades.  Such costs 
would include, for instance, shared network upgrades included in the CAISO’s TPP process to 
serve data center load growth, which could result in cost shifting to other transmission customers 
if the Large Load Interconnection Customer’s load does not materialize as expected.  SCE 
reconciles the MARO annually.  If the customer’s retail transmission revenues exceed the 
MARO, nothing further is due.  Otherwise, SCE invoices the shortfall, payable within 30 
Calendar Days, and the payment is treated as revenue providing a Transmission Revenue Credit 
in SCE’s Formula Rate process, which reduces the risk of transmission cost shifting to other 
transmission customers.  The customer must also post security for the MARO equal to five years 
of the MARO at full capacity.  If a shortfall goes unpaid for 60 Calendar Days, SCE may draw 
on that security, and the customer must replenish it within 30 Calendar Days. 
Third, the customer bears the cost of prematurely terminating service through exit fees.  
Before service begins, a customer that fails to take service owes a Withdrawal Penalty equal to 
the greater of 20% of its estimated Large Load Network Upgrade costs or the actual cost of 
abandoned facilities (TSRA Appendix B; LLIP Section 3.10.1(c)).  After service begins, early 
termination triggers an Exit Fee equal to all remaining required security, including the MARO 
security, due within 30 Business Days, and SCE may draw on the security if it is not paid.   
 
15 The 80% multiplier approximates the capacity factor of the Large Load, and the 75% multiplier 
represents the minimum payment threshold.
--- PAGE 25 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission

November 16, 2026

Finally, in order to avoid creating duplicative credit support requirements, Section 
11.4.2.4 provides that Large Load Interconnection Customer may avoid duplicative credit 
requirements by demonstrating to Participating TO that security or payments provided pursuant 
to a retail agreement with the Large Load Owner satisfies some portion of Large Load 
Interconnection Customer’s obligations.16 
In the absence of CAISO direction on cost-shifting mitigation, SCE believes that it is 
important for the various PTOs to align, to the extent possible, on the approach to mitigation in 
order to ensure consistency across the CAISO territory.  As noted, SCE understands that its cost 
shifting mitigation proposal shares key features with those of other PTOs. PG&E in particular is 
proposing substantively similar cost-shifting mitigation measures in its own tariff revisions, 
including an additional minimum revenue obligation intended to cover broader network upgrades 
above and beyond those Large Load Network Upgrades triggered by the Large Load.  In 
response to the Commission’s Briefing Question at P 125, SCE believes that its proposed 
approach provides the appropriate structure and minimum level of protection to prevent unjust 
and unreasonable cost shifts among transmission customers related to network upgrade costs 
required for large loads at this time.  SCE also supports the CAISO’s commitment to explore 
additional cost-shifting protection for non-FERC-jurisdictional PTOs in a future stakeholder 
process.17

C.  FLEXIBLE SERVICE 
In Section D of the Order (“Extending New Transmission Services to Eligible Customers 
Taking Service on Behalf of Flexible Large Loads”), the Commission preliminarily found that 
the CAISO tariff may be unjust and unreasonable because it does not include transmission 
services that reflect Eligible Customers taking transmission service on behalf of flexible large 
loads that are willing and able to limit their use of the transmission system under certain 
conditions.18  The Order directed the CAISO to explain whether its Tariff remains just and 
reasonable without the transmission services described by the Commission in the PJM Co-
Location Order, with regional variation as appropriate, being available to Eligible Customers 
taking transmission service on behalf of flexible large loads, or propose appropriate Tariff 
revisions.   
As stated in the CAISO Draft Final Proposal, the CAISO is not proposing to create new 
“transmission services” or create more responsibilities around Eligible Customers, which would 
require redesigning transmission and interconnection service entirely.19  The CAISO does not 
believe such a redesign is warranted, and the Commission expressly invited the CAISO to 
explain alternative service frameworks that may enable similar flexibility.  Instead, the CAISO 
proposes to create new, flexible interim interconnection service options that will enable large 
loads and co-located loads to limit their energy withdrawals or injections until the needed 
network upgrades are complete.  The CAISO’s proposed name for this service is Flexible Interim 
Load Interconnection.  The CAISO Draft Final Proposal indicated that PTOs will have the 
primary role in defining and implementing this interconnection service offering.20  For instance, 
CAISO has not proposed a pro forma FILI agreement as part of its tariff revisions.  Instead, the 
 
18  Order at P 104. 
19  CAISO Draft Final Proposal, p. 67. 
20  Id. at p. 66.
--- PAGE 27 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 27 
November 16, 2026  
 
27 
CAISO tariff revisions indicate that each PTO will have its own pro forma agreement to 
memorialize the terms of any FILI arrangement.   
In the CAISO Draft Final Proposal, the CAISO indicated that it sees value in 
development of permanent flexible service approach but defers the permanent offering to a 
future initiative.21  SCE concurs that there is value to developing a permanent flexible service 
and agrees that the CAISO will need to play a lead role in the development of that proposal.  
SCE continues to see customer benefits in flexibility and will actively participate in the CAISO’s 
future stakeholder process to develop that permanent flexible service.  
Although the Order did not direct SCE, as a PTO, to provide an interim flexible service 
for large loads, SCE is supporting the CAISO’s proposed FILI in this filing, including by 
proposing its own pro forma FILI agreement.   
1. 
Tariff Changes to Implement New Flexible Interim Interconnection Service 
SCE proposes tariff changes to implement FILI, which the LLIP defines as curtailable, 
interim transmission interconnection service for a Large Load, provided by the CAISO and the 
Participating TO, that enables the Large Load to interconnect before completion of specified 
Large Load Network Upgrades, Distribution Upgrades, or the Participating TO’s Interconnection 
Facilities.  Specifically, SCE proposes to offer FILI through Sections 4.6.2 and 4.6.2.1 of the 
LLIP, Section 9.7 and Appendix E of the TSRA, and the new FILI Agreement. 
To obtain FILI, a large load would first request interconnection under the LLIP.  If the 
90-day Large Load Interconnection Study finds that the full amount of the large load’s 
interconnection request cannot be accommodated without transmission network upgrades, the 
large load may request a FILI Study to determine (i) whether the Large Load may interconnect 
and begin receiving limited service before all upgrades are completed; (ii) the conditions and 
 
21  Id. at p. 68.
--- PAGE 28 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 28 
November 16, 2026  
 
28 
limitations of such service; (iii) the upgrades and the associated cost; and (iv) the estimated 
duration required to provide the limited service (LLIP Section 4.6.2). 
The Large Load Interconnection Customer may request a FILI Study after the Large 
Load Interconnection Study.  If the customer elects to proceed with a FILI Study, the 
Participating TO will complete the study within 60 Calendar Days (LLIP Section 4.6.2).  The 
Participating TO will perform the FILI Study, and the Large Load Interconnection Customer will 
be responsible for the actual costs of the study.  If at any time the Participating TO determines 
that it will not meet the required time frame for completing the FILI Study, it shall notify the 
customer of the schedule status and provide an estimated completion date with an explanation of 
the reasons why additional time is required.  The Participating TO will share the results with the 
CAISO for review and concurrence, and if the customer requests it within 10 Business Days after 
the FILI Study report is issued, the Participating TO, the CAISO, and the customer will hold a 
Report Meeting (LLIP Section 4.6.2).   
As detailed in Section 9.8 and Appendix E of the TSRA and in the FILI Agreement 
(Attachment 4 to the LLIP), large loads with FILI will be subject to the operational requirements 
to ensure that they do not cause reliability impacts on the grid, including potential curtailment.  
Once transmission network upgrades are completed, the Large Load will be able to have its full 
load served (i.e., receive firm service). 
SCE understands other PTOs are also presenting similar FILI proposals in their tariff 
revisions. 
D.  PROXIMATE LARGE LOAD AND CO-LOCATED LOAD 
In Section E (“Interconnection Customers Serving Electrically Proximate Large Load and 
Co-Located Load”), the Order found that that the CAISO tariff appears to be unjust and 
unreasonable because it does not contain provisions allowing an interconnection customer to 
seek the generator interconnection service(s) that reflects the operational dynamics of serving
--- PAGE 29 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 29 
November 16, 2026  
 
29 
either: (1) an electrically proximate large load; or (2) a co-located load, as defined in the Order, 
that has a high peak load (i.e., large co-located loads that are 50 MW or greater).22 The 
Commission defined an “electrically proximate large load” as a large load “sufficiently 
electrically close to the interconnection customer’s requested point of interconnection, such that 
the impact on the transmission system of the combination of the generating facility and the load, 
with the exception of the transmission facilities between the two, will be effectively the same as 
if they were located at the same substation (e.g., large load that is located no more than two 
substations from the generating facility).”23  The Order did not direct SCE, as a PTO, to take any 
action regarding proximate large loads. 
In the CAISO Draft Final Proposal, the CAISO proposes considering provisions for 
interconnection customers to serve electrically proximate large loads in a future stakeholder 
initiative.24  The CAISO stated that it intends to prioritize the more common stand-alone and co-
located load and generator interconnections with this proposal and filing and will focus on 
proximate load and generation arrangements once the process for co-location is underway.  SCE 
supports the CAISO’s proposal to consider provision regarding proximate load in a future 
stakeholder initiative.   
In Section C (“Co-Location Arrangements and Load with Behind the Meter Generation”), 
the Commission preliminarily found the CAISO tariff to be deficient as it does not currently 
address the rates, terms, and conditions that apply to co-location arrangements.25  SCE supports 
the CAISO proposal to clarify how co-located large loads and generation may interconnect to 
and operate within the CAISO-controlled grid and will work with the CAISO to implement this 
proposal.  SCE agrees with CAISO that the PTOs will continue to govern and study all load

interconnection requests pursuant to their tariffs, and the CAISO will continue to govern and

that may export energy to the transmission grid will require a CAISO interconnection study

The Commission also posed several Briefing Questions regarding co-located and 
proximate loads.  In response to the Commission’s Briefing Question at P 124 regarding the 
impacts of new transmission services, SCE emphasizes that neither the CAISO nor SCE are 
proposing any new transmission services.  Because no new transmission services are being 
proposed, there will be no impact of the introduction of new transmission services on 
transmission planning in the CAISO.  Of note, neither the CAISO nor the PTOs are proposing 
“permanent flexibility” at this time.  The CAISO has committed to an additional stakeholder 
process to explore the details on what “permanent flexibility” could encompass.26  SCE supports 
further exploration of permanent flexibility that has the potential to provide customer benefits 
including reduced transmission costs (for both the interconnecting customer and likely other 
transmission customers), as well as potential reliability benefits.  Implementation of permanent 
flexibility is likely to impact multiple aspects of the transmission process, including 
interconnection, operations and planning.  However, until the CAISO concludes this future 
process, it is premature to describe its impacts on the transmission planning process. 
With respect to the Briefing Questions at P 127 concerning co-located and proximate 
generators’ participation in the energy and ancillary services market and considerations 
regarding resource adequacy, SCE notes that the CAISO Tariff governs both participation in the 
CAISO’s energy and ancillary services market and how generating facilities count for the

purposes of resource adequacy.  Accordingly, SCE believes that these questions are best

1. 
Tariff Changes to Address Co-Located Load 
As discussed above in Section II.A, all new Large Loads will be required to comply with 
the application and study procedures in the LLIP, as well as the operational requirements in the 
TSRA.  This will include any Large Loads with non-exporting co-located generation.  
Additionally, SCE has incorporated specific provisions to address the Commission’s concerns 
about co-located loads, including:  
• Scope of LLIP study for non-exporting co-located generation. Co-
located generation serving a Large Load that will not export to the CAISO 
Controlled Grid is evaluated for impacts on SCE’s Transmission System 
as part of the LLIP study process, provided it is included in the 
Interconnection Request (LLIP Section 3.1).  The Large Load 
Interconnection Study identifies the impacts of the Large Load and the 
incremental impacts of non-exporting co-located generation that is not 
subject to short circuit duty dependence (LLIP Section 4.6.1.1). 
• Exporting co-located generation. Co-located generation that will export 
to the CAISO Controlled Grid must submit a separate generator 
interconnection request to the CAISO under Section 25.7.6 and Appendix 
KK of the CAISO Tariff, while the Large Load itself is evaluated under 
the LLIP (LLIP Section 3.1; LLIP Attachment 1). 
• Interconnection Request form.  LLIP Attachment 1 (Interconnection 
Request form)  requires a description of any co-located generation, 
including whether it will export, its proposed date of initial operation, and
--- PAGE 32 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 32 
November 16, 2026  
 
32 
gross Large Load and gross co-located generation reported separately (not 
netted); maximum net withdrawal with the generation online; the largest 
co-located unit or storage device that could trip; and firm and curtailable 
withdrawal requested when the generation is unavailable (which cannot 
exceed the requested maximum load).  It also requires non-export control 
information, including ride-through capability and protection settings, the 
expected response of the generation and load to a sudden loss of either, 
protection coordination, and disturbance monitoring. 
• Technical data. Large Loads co-locating with non-exporting generation 
must complete the applicable generator data in Appendix A to LLIP 
Attachment 1 (synchronous, induction, wind, or inverter-based), including 
power flow, dynamic and, if required, EMT models. 
• Inverter standards. New LLIP Section 3.11 requires inverters in co-
located generating facilities, including storage that later injects electricity, 
to meet UL-1741 and UL-1741 Supplement B certification. 
• Study base case. The Base Case used for the Interconnection Studies may 
include Large Loads and any co-located generation (LLIP Section 2). 
• Cost responsibility and security. Under TSRA Sections 11.3.1 and 
11.4.2.2, the customer bears the full actual cost of, and must post security 
at least 30 Calendar Days before work begins on, Large Load Network 
Upgrades attributable to non-export co-located generation.  Where the 
customer is SCE’s retail transmission customer, cost assignment and 
security follow SCE’s retail tariffs, subject to the Local Regulatory 
Authority.
--- PAGE 33 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 33 
November 16, 2026  
 
33 
• Construction, testing and operation. The TSRA extends to co-located 
generation, where applicable, the as-built drawing and data requirements 
(Section 5.6.3); pre- and post-service testing, parallel-operation approval 
and right to observe testing (Sections 6.1–6.3); operation and maintenance 
obligations and coordination (Sections 9.4, 10.2, 10.3); synchronization 
(Section 9.5); and system protection and fault-removal requirements 
(Sections 9.7.4.1 and 9.7.5).  The customer may not operate the co-located 
generation in parallel with SCE’s system without SCE’s prior written 
approval. 
• Metering and changes in data. “Metering Equipment” includes metering 
at co-located generation, and the customer may not energize or operate the 
Large Load or co-located generation until SCE completes studies when the 
customer’s actual data differs materially from that originally provided 
(TSRA Article 24.3). 
E.  SUMMARY OF OTHER PROPOSED TARIFF CHANGES 
In addition to the proposed changes described above, SCE is proposing revisions to the 
body of its TO Tariff that are necessary to incorporate the new LLIP and clarify certain 
provisions.  These changes are summarized below. 
i. 
The Table of Contents has been revised to add a new appendix, Appendix XV (Large 
Load Interconnection Procedures) and its Attachments 1 through 4. 
ii. 
Sections 1 and 1.3 have been modified to clarify the entities that provide retail 
transmission service that may apply for Large Loads interconnections.  
iii. 
The introduction of Section 3 has been revised to account for defined terms in 
Appendix XV.  SCE has also revised the definition of a Transmission Revenue Credit 
to confirm that any remaining Interconnection Financial Security under the TSRA 
that has been drawn down by Participating TO will be included as a Transmission 
Revenue Credit in calculating SCE’s Transmission Revenue Balancing Account 
Adjustment.
--- PAGE 34 ---
Ms. Debbie-Anne A. Reese, Secretary 
Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
Page 34 
November 16, 2026  
 
34 
iv. 
Section 4 has been revised to clarify Eligible Customers who qualify for transmission 
service under the CAISO Tariff.  
v. 
Section 7.1 has been revised to incorporate Appendix XV. 
vi. 
Section 8.1 has been revised to incorporate the interconnection of Large Loads as a 
Participating TO obligation as governed by Appendix XV. 
vii. 
Section 9.1 has been revised to indicate that expansions on behalf of Large Loads are 
governed by Appendix XV.   
viii. 
Section 10.1 has been revised to indicate that requests for interconnection on behalf 
of Large Loads are governed by Appendix XV rather than the existing wholesale load 
procedures. 
ix. 
Section 13.2 has been revised to reference Appendix XV, as applicable.

1.  Preamble.  
The Participating TO's revenue requirements and applicable rates and charges for 
transmission access over the ISO Controlled Grid and the terms and conditions for 
transmission expansion and interconnection are set forth in this TO Tariff and the ISO 
Tariff. 
1.1  Transmission Access for Participating TOs. 
Participating TOs are able to participate in the ISO and utilize the entire ISO Controlled 
Grid to serve their End-Use Customers.  The applicable High Voltage Access Charges 
and Transition Charges shall be paid by Participating TOs to the ISO pursuant to the 
ISO Tariff.  If a Participating TO utilizes the Low Voltage Transmission Facilities of 
another Participating TO, such Participating TO shall also pay the Low Voltage Access 
Charge of the other Participating TO.  
1.2  Transmission Access for Wheeling Customers. 
Wheeling allows Scheduling Coordinators to deliver Energy through or out of the ISO 
Controlled Grid to serve a load located outside the transmission or Distribution System 
of a Participating TO.  Wheeling Access Charges shall be paid by Scheduling 
Coordinators to the ISO pursuant to the ISO Tariff. 
1.3  Transmission Access for End-Users.   
End-Users receive transmission service over the ISO Controlled Grid through the Utility 
Distribution Company, Small Utility Distribution Company, or MSS Operator to whose 
transmission or distribution facilities the End-User is directly connected.  Charges to 
End-Users for access to the ISO Controlled Grid shall be paid to the applicable Utility
--- PAGE 42 ---
Southern California Edison Company   
 
  
FERC Electric Tariff, Third Revised Volume No. 6  
 
 
 
 
 
Distribution Company, Small Utility Distribution Company, or MSS Operator to whose 
transmission or distribution facilities the End-User is directly connected.
--- PAGE 43 ---
Southern California Edison Company   
 
  
FERC Electric Tariff, Third Revised Volume No. 6  
 
 
 
 
 
 
3.  TO Definitions. 
Capitalized terms used in this TO Tariff shall have the meanings set out below or in 
Appendix XV unless otherwise stated.  Capitalized terms used in this tariff and not 
defined below or in Appendix XV shall have the meanings set out in the ISO Tariff. 
3.1 
Access Charge.  A charge paid by all UDCs, MSSs and, in certain cases, 
Scheduling Coordinators, delivering Energy to Gross Load, as set forth in Section 26.1 
of the ISO Tariff.  The Access Charge includes the High Voltage Access Charge, the 
Transition Charge, and the Low Voltage Access Charge. 
3.2 
Automatic Generation Control (AGC).  Generation equipment that 
automatically responds to signals from the ISO’s EMS control in real time to control the 
power output of electric generators within a prescribed area in response to a change in 
system frequency, tieline loading, or the relation of these to each other, so as to 
maintain the target system frequency and/or the established interchange with other 
areas within the predetermined limits. 
3.3 
Ancillary Services.  Regulation, Spinning Reserve, Non-Spinning Reserve, 
Voltage Support and Black Start together with such other interconnected operation 
services as the ISO may develop in cooperation with Market Participants to support the 
transmission of Energy from Generation resources to Loads while maintaining reliable 
operation of the ISO Controlled Grid in accordance with Good Utility Practice.

                           Version Number: 16.0.0 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Option Code:  A
